LinkedIn Comments:
=====================
I'd add observability and governance to the underwater architecture. Production AI isn't just about whether an agent completes a task. It's about understanding why it made a decision, what data it accessed, which tools it invoked, how much it cost, and whether it stayed within policy. As agents become more autonomous, telemetry, auditability, runtime security, and evaluation frameworks become as critical as the models themselves. We spent decades building reliability into distributed systems. AI agents deserve the same engineering discipline, just with a lot more probability sprinkled in to keep everyone humble.

rust in autonomous agents will come as much from transparency and governance as from raw intelligence. 

I'd definitely add cost and latency guardrails to that underwater architecture. In production, an agent loop can easily go rogue, burning thousands of dollars in tokens or hitting rate limits in minutes. If you don't have circuit breakers for cost and infinite loops, your ship sinks before it even hits the storm. Great write-up.

The keel-versus-wind framing lands. Your three prerequisites are all upstream setup: spec before execution, decomposition before autonomy, context before long-running work. A long-running agent drifts from even a good spec, and the drift is locally reasonable each step, so it doesn't announce itself. Engineering depth on the front end plus guardrails on the back end is what separates a keel from a demo that floated once.… more

The first documented case of agentic ransomware is here.
And the most important part is not that AI wrote malware.

It is that the AI appears to have adapted when things went wrong.

Sysdig’s Threat Research Team documented what it assesses to be the first known case of agentic ransomware: JADEPUFFER.

The attack chain is worth studying.

It started with an internet-facing Langflow instance.

From there, the operation:

1. Exploited CVE-2025-3248 to gain Python code execution

2. Ran reconnaissance across the host, network, and processes

3. Searched for LLM API keys, cloud credentials, database secrets, wallets, and configuration files

4. Dumped Langflow’s backing Postgres database

5. Scanned reachable internal services

6. Found MinIO and used default credentials to enumerate buckets and retrieve sensitive files

7. Established persistence through a cron-based beacon

8. Pivoted toward the real production target running MySQL and Nacos

9. Attempted multiple takeover paths, created a backdoor admin, encrypted 1,342 Nacos configuration items, dropped original tables, and created a ransom note

But this is the part every AI architect should pay attention to:

The operation did not simply execute a fixed script.

According to Sysdig, when one Nacos login attempt failed, the system diagnosed the problem, changed its approach, rebuilt the admin account, and reached a successful login.

The reported window from failure to corrective fix:

31 seconds.

That is the architectural shift.

Traditional automation:

Plan → Execute → Fail → Stop

Agentic attack execution:

Plan → Act → Observe → Diagnose → Adjust → Retry → Continue

The individual techniques were not necessarily new.

The orchestration was.

An agent could chain together:

Reconnaissance
→ Secret discovery
→ Credential harvesting
→ Internal discovery
→ Persistence
→ Lateral movement
→ Exploitation
→ Self-correction
→ Destruction

This should change how we think about AI security.

We have spent enormous energy discussing:

Prompt injection
Model safety
Jailbreaks
Guardrails
Output filtering

All important.

But production agents introduce another problem:

Autonomy changes the economics and speed of attack execution.

A vulnerable AI application is not just another vulnerable web server.

It may sit close to:

LLM provider keys
Cloud credentials
Vector databases
Object stores
MCP servers
Internal APIs
Service accounts
Data pipelines
Production systems

That makes AI infrastructure a potentially powerful pivot point.

For  architecture teams, I think the security model now needs to expand:

Identity boundaries for agents
Least-privilege tool access
Network segmentation around AI infrastructure
Runtime behavioral monitoring
Secret isolation
Short-lived credentials
Human approval for destructive actions
Detection of autonomous retry loops
Kill switches outside the agent’s control

Source: Sysdig Threat Research Team, JADEPUFFER analysis, July 2026.

12h


The important shift here is not that AI can be used in an attack.
It is that autonomy changes the speed and economics of execution.
Traditional automation usually fails and stops. Agentic systems can observe, adjust, retry, and continue. That same capability that makes agents useful in business workflows also changes the risk profile when the system is misused or poorly secured.
This is why AI infrastructure cannot be treated like a normal application layer.
Agents need identity boundaries, least-privilege tool access, isolated secrets, runtime monitoring, approval gates for high-impact actions, and shutdown paths outside the agent’s control.
The enterprise question is no longer only “can the agent complete the task?”
It is “can we control what happens when the agent keeps trying?”… more

Hina Arora Verified Profile FollowingHina Arora  • Following

31m


Excellent analysis Brij Agentic AI security must evolve from protecting models to controlling autonomy, permissions, and real-world actions.

I used to think AI only got better when humans kept correcting it.

Then I learned about reinforcement learning.

It reminded me of how we learn in real life.

You don't become better by reading a hundred books.

You become better by making decisions, getting feedback, and trying again.

That's exactly what reinforcement learning is.

An AI agent takes an action.

It gets rewarded if the outcome is good.

It gets penalized if it's not.

Over thousands (or millions) of attempts, it discovers what actually works.

The interesting part?

The same idea applies to our careers.

Every project, every mistake, every conversation is feedback.

The people who improve the fastest aren't the ones who avoid failure—they're the ones who learn from it.

Technology and life have more in common than we often realize.

Follow me S Rajeshwari for more about Agentic AI


🗺 𝟭𝟬 𝗧𝗶𝗺𝗲 𝗦𝗲𝗿𝗶𝗲𝘀 𝗠𝗼𝗱𝗲𝗹𝘀 𝗘𝘃𝗲𝗿𝘆 𝗗𝗮𝘁𝗮 𝗦𝗰𝗶𝗲𝗻𝘁𝗶𝘀𝘁 𝗠𝘂𝘀𝘁 𝗞𝗻𝗼𝘄 
𝐸𝑠𝑠𝑒𝑛𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙 𝑇𝑖𝑚𝑒 𝑆𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑠 𝐹𝑜𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑎𝑠𝑡𝑖𝑛𝑔 𝐶ℎ𝑒𝑎𝑡 𝑆ℎ𝑒𝑒𝑡

Time series forecasting powers everything from stock markets and weather prediction to demand forecasting, IoT monitoring, anomaly detection, and AI-driven business analytics. Choosing the right model isn't just about accuracy; it's about understanding your data's trend, seasonality, stationarity, and noise.
This cheat sheet summarizes 10 of the most important time series models, explaining when to use each one, the intuition behind it, and their key trade-offs:
1️⃣ 𝗠𝗼𝘃𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗔𝘃𝗲𝗿𝗮𝗴𝗲 (𝗠𝗔𝘃𝗲) 
→ Smooths short-term fluctuations using the average of recent observations.
2️⃣ 𝗘𝘅𝗽𝗼𝗻𝗲𝗻𝘁𝗶𝗮𝗹 𝗦𝗺𝗼𝗼𝘁𝗵𝗶𝗻𝗴 (𝗘𝗦) 
→ Assigns more weight to recent observations for responsive forecasting.
3️⃣ 𝗧𝗶𝗺𝗲 𝗦𝗲𝗿𝗶𝗲𝘀 𝗗𝗲𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗽𝗼𝘀𝗶𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻 
→ Separates data into trend, seasonality, cyclical components, and noise.
4️⃣ 𝗔𝘂𝘁𝗼𝗿𝗲𝗴𝗿𝗲𝘀𝘀𝗶𝘃𝗲 (𝗔𝗥) 
→ Predicts future values using previous observations.
5️⃣ 𝗠𝗼𝘃𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗔𝘃𝗲𝗿𝗮𝗴𝗲 (𝗠𝗔) 
→ Models future values using previous forecast errors (shocks).
6️⃣ 𝗔𝗥𝗠𝗔 (𝗽, 𝗾) 
→ Combines autoregression with moving-average error modeling for stationary series.
7️⃣ 𝗔𝗥𝗜𝗠𝗔 (𝗽, 𝗱, 𝗾) 
→ Extends ARMA by using differencing to model non-stationary data.
8️⃣ 𝗦𝗔𝗥𝗜𝗠𝗔 (𝗽,𝗱,𝗾)(𝗣,𝗗,𝗤)ₛ 
→ Captures both trend and recurring seasonal patterns.
9️⃣ 𝗛𝗼𝗹𝘁-𝗪𝗶𝗻𝘁𝗲𝗿𝘀 (𝗘𝗧𝗦) 
→ Triple exponential smoothing that models level, trend, and seasonality simultaneously.
🔟 𝗣𝗿𝗼𝗽𝗵𝗲𝘁 (𝗠𝗲𝘁𝗮) 
→ Automated forecasting model designed for business data with holidays, missing values, outliers, and changepoints.

𝗤𝘂𝗶𝗰𝗸 𝗥𝘂𝗹𝗲 𝗼𝗳 𝗧𝗵𝘂𝗺𝗯
✅ Stable data → Moving Average / Exponential Smoothing
✅ Stationary series → AR, MA, ARMA
✅ Trending data → ARIMA
✅ Seasonal data → SARIMA or Holt-Winters
✅ Business forecasting with holidays & irregular events → Prophet
Understanding these classical forecasting models is still incredibly valuable; even in the era of Transformers and foundation models.
Many production forecasting systems continue to rely on ARIMA, SARIMA, ETS, or Prophet because they are interpretable, data-efficient, and computationally inexpensive.


You built a RAG system. It worked perfectly in testing. Then it hit production and started hallucinating, retrieving irrelevant docs, and giving wrong answers.

The dirty secret about RAG: the basic version everyone starts with fails predictably.

A user asks "How do I reset my password?" and your system retrieves a network-security doc — because both mention "authentication."

The gap between a demo and production RAG is massive. But the gap between bad and bulletproof RAG is small — if you know which pattern fixes each failure 👇

10 methods to make Advanced RAG:

1. Query Rewriting — generate 3–5 rewrites so users and docs "speak the same language." (20–40% recall, 1.2× cost)

2. Hybrid Retrieval — dense vectors + BM25 keyword search, merged with Reciprocal Rank Fusion. (15–25% recall, the industry standard)

3. Reranking — a cross-encoder rescoring the top candidates. The single highest-ROI step. (≈30% accuracy: 70% → 85–88%)

4. Contextual Compression — keep only the sentences relevant to the query. (70–90% fewer generation tokens)

5. Parent Document Retrieval — match small chunks, return the full parent for context.

6. Self-Query Retrieval — LLM extracts metadata filters (team, year, category) from natural language. (60–80% noise reduction)

7. HyDE — generate a hypothetical answer, embed it, then retrieve real docs that match it. Great when question and answer use different words.

8. Multi-Query Expansion — ask the question 3–5 ways at once, pool and dedupe.

9. Feedback Loops — self-correcting RAG that recognises gaps and retrieves again (2–3 rounds).

10. Agentic RAG — let the LLM decide whether to retrieve at all.

The decision framework:
Start with hybrid + reranking + basic query rewriting → 70% to 85% accuracy at 1.4–1.5× cost. That alone solves 80% of production RAG problems.

Then add ONLY for a measured failure mode:
• Irrelevant chunks → compression
• Missing context → parent docs
• Metadata filtering → self-query
• Technical queries → HyDE
• Multi-hop → feedback loops
• Mixed conversations → agentic

The best RAG system isn't the most sophisticated one. It's the one your users trust enough to rely on every single day.

Which patterns are you running in production? 👇


Claude Code is easier when you explain it like Lego.

𝗕𝗲𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗲 𝗯𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗮𝘁 𝗔𝗜 𝗶𝗻 𝗷𝘂𝘀𝘁 𝟭 𝗺𝗶𝗻𝘂𝘁𝗲 𝗮 𝗱𝗮𝘆.
𝗚𝗲𝘁 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗔𝗜 𝗻𝗲𝘄𝘀𝗹𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝘀𝗺𝗮𝗿𝘁 𝗹𝗲𝗮𝗱𝗲𝗿𝘀 𝗿𝗲𝗮𝗱.
𝗦𝗶𝗴𝗻 𝘂𝗽 𝗳𝗿𝗲𝗲 𝗻𝗼𝘄 → aiforleaders.com
__________

Here’s the simple version:

Claude Code is not just a chatbot.

It is more like a tiny coding helper that can read files, write code, check its work, and follow rules.

But the names make it sound harder than it is.

So explain it like this:

💻 Terminal & CLI

This is Claude’s playground.

It is the black screen where you type commands and Claude does the work.

🤖 Auto Mode

This is when Claude keeps going without asking every 10 seconds.

Useful when the job is clear and low risk.

🧠 Plan Mode

This is Claude saying:

“Before I build the Lego castle, here is my plan.”

Use this before big code changes.

✅ Permissions

This is Claude asking:

“Am I allowed to do this?”

It stops risky actions before they happen.

🎒 Context Window

This is Claude’s school bag.

It can only carry so many files, chats, and instructions at once.

→ /compact

This squashes the chat smaller.

Use it when Claude’s school bag is getting too full.

→ /effort

This tells Claude how hard to think.

Low effort for simple fixes.
High effort for tricky problems.

→ /btw

This is a quick side note.

Use it when you want to tell Claude something without stopping the main task.

→ /loop

This is Claude setting a timer.

It keeps checking the same thing again and again.

📄 CLAUDE.md

This is the rule book.

It tells Claude how your project works before it touches the code.

🧰 Skills

These are instruction packs.

Load one, and Claude suddenly knows the right way to do a specific job.

🧑‍🔧 Sub-agents

These are helper Claudes.

One can check bugs.
One can write tests.
One can review the code.

👷 Agent Teams

This is a group project.

Each agent gets one job so the work does not get messy.

🌳 Worktrees

This is a spare copy of your code.

Claude can try things there without breaking the real version.

📸 Git Commits

These are save points.

Like saving a game before fighting the boss.

🔌 MCP Servers

These connect Claude to tools like Notion, GitHub, Slack, or your data.

⚙️ Hooks

These are automatic helpers.

For example, run a code checker every time Claude saves a file.

🗡 Slash Commands

These are shortcuts.

Type one command and Claude knows what job to start.

📝 Custom Commands

These are shortcuts you create yourself.

Teach Claude once, then reuse it every week.

⏰ Claude Routines

These are scheduled jobs.

Claude can run a task while you are asleep.

The point:

You do not need all 20 features today.

Start with these 6:

→ Plan Mode
→ Permissions
→ CLAUDE.md
→ Git Commits
→ /compact
→ Sub-agents

That covers most real work.

The rest matters once your projects get bigger.

Which Claude Code feature would you try first?


​🚀 Navigating the ever-evolving landscape of AI Multi-Agent Frameworks?

​Choosing the right tool can make or break your production pipeline. To make things easier, I've put together a visual breakdown of the top contenders for 2026: 
Google ADK, LangChain, LangGraph, LangFlow, and LangSmith.

​Here is a quick cheat sheet on where each framework truly shines:

​🔹 Google ADK: The go-to for enterprise-grade, production agent orchestration with explicit parallel and sequential workflow control.

🔹 LangChain: The ultimate AI framework for rapid prototyping, boasting 500+ integrations and modular components.

🔹 LangGraph: The perfect orchestrator for stateful, complex, and cyclic agent workflows with checkpointing.

🔹 LangFlow: A fantastic visual builder for no-code pipeline development, ideal for rapid demos and visual prototyping.

🔹 LangSmith: Your observability powerhouse for full trace replays, production debugging, and rigorous dataset evaluation.

​When architecting robust AI solutions, understanding the unique strengths of each component is crucial for building scalable and efficient systems.

​Which framework are you currently exploring or leaning heavily on for your AI projects?

For a long time, I thought Deep Learning was just about building bigger neural networks.

More layers.

More parameters.

More GPUs.

But the more production systems I worked on, the more I realized that model architecture is only a small part of the problem.

A model that scores 99% in a notebook can completely fail in production.

Not because the model is bad.

Because real-world data is messy.

Missing values.

Distribution shifts.

Class imbalance.

Noisy labels.

Latency constraints.

Limited compute.

Changing user behavior.

That's where Deep Learning becomes engineering—not experimentation.

Over time, I learned a few lessons that changed how I approach every project:
• Spend more time understanding your data than tuning your model.
• Start with the simplest architecture that solves the problem.
• Build a reliable data pipeline before chasing benchmark accuracy.
• Measure precision, recall, latency, and business impact—not just accuracy.
• Monitor models continuously because today's best model can become 
tomorrow's weakest one.

The most valuable Deep Learning skill isn't memorizing architectures like CNNs, Transformers, or Diffusion Models.

It's understanding why a model succeeds, why it fails, and how to make it reliable in production.

Deep Learning is no longer about building impressive demos.

It's about building systems that people can trust.

What's the biggest lesson Deep Learning has taught you?
